cross Asymmetric On-Policy Distillation Learns In-Context Exploration

Authors: Sriyash Poddar, Yanda Bao, Jacob Krantz, Matthew Chang, Xavier Puig, Roozbeh Mottaghi, Natasha Jaques, Abhishek Gupta

Abstract: Sequential decision-making under partial observability is difficult because agents must explore to infer hidden, task-relevant information before acting effectively. Learning such information-gathering behaviors tabula rasa via online reinforcement learning is sample-inefficient, whereas offline imitation averages expert actions across hidden states and fails to learn exploration. In this work, we propose ASTEROID, a simple and effective student--teacher supervised learning framework that can learn test-time exploration. Although a privileged expert never needs to explore, we demonstrate that a history-conditioned student can learn to do so when trained with on-policy contexts: the student first collects on-policy context under partial observability, and the privileged expert provides action labels conditioned on that context. By distilling expert actions directly conditioned on the student's on-policy history, the student learns to systematically resolve partial observability, i.e., search while uncertain and exploit once the hidden state is revealed. This enables efficient test-time exploration (similar to Bayesian posterior sampling) without incurring the impractical training-data costs of reinforcement learning. Across a diverse range of eleven different environments, including discrete exploration benchmarks, robotic manipulation, navigation, and Procgen environments, ASTEROID achieves ~2x higher returns under fixed exploration budgets, uses ~100x fewer interactions than RL baselines, and outperforms frontier models at exploration. Finally, our method transfers policies trained in simulation to vision-denied real-world robot manipulation, exhibiting efficient exploration and increasing success by +40% over standard distillation. Overall, it provides a simple yet surprisingly effective framework to learn efficient test-time exploration.

cross Multimodal Embodied Agents: A Unified Capability-centric Perspective from Computer-Use to Robot-Use

Authors: Yanzhe Chen, Ziyi Yang, Jifeng Zhu, Qiming Huang, Ruihe An, Peiyao Xu, Hesen Yang, Runda Liu, Chang Gong, Zhijun Cao, Zechen Bai, Wenzheng Zeng, Yiqi Lin, Guoqiang Liang, Kevin Yuchen Ma, Kevin Qinghong Lin, Mike Zheng Shou

Abstract: Multimodal agents (MMAs) sustain interaction through reasoning, memory, tools, and feedback, most visibly as computer-use agents, while robotic systems (RSs) couple sensing and actuation under physical dynamics. We define multimodal embodied agents (MMEAs) as goal-directed systems that couple multimodal task reasoning with physical action and revise decisions from the resulting feedback. This raises a central question: what changes when a multimodal agent moves from computer-use to robot-use? We introduce PAPAV, a unified capability-centric framework of five recurring functions: Perceive the current state, Anticipate action effects, Plan a feasible course, Act through an interface or body, and Verify the outcome. Defined by function rather than architecture, these capabilities let PAPAV identify what all three share and then compare MMEAs with MMAs and with RSs. Five physical constraints, from partial observability to unverifiable outcomes, leave fewer choices fixed in advance and less room to reverse errors. Across 64 benchmarks, explicit evaluation centers on Act; only 5 assess Anticipate and 3 assess Verify, leaving both largely hidden behind task success. These constraints also frame the open challenges we identify. PAPAV therefore provides a common basis for designing reliable physical agents and measuring progress across all five capabilities rather than task success alone.

cross Quant-Arena: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Quantitative Trading Research

Authors: Houde Qian, Zheng Jiang, Wenyi Li, Chenxi Xu, Xinjie Lin, Yuzhao Peng, Ruoyu Han, Youjie Zheng, Kaisen Yang, Qinhuai Na, You Fu, Calvin Xiao

Abstract: Quantitative research is an iterative loop: a researcher proposes a market hypothesis, implements it as an executable signal, tests it on historical data, and decides what to keep. Existing benchmarks score formulas, code, or individual trading decisions, leaving open whether large language models (LLMs) can complete this loop and produce strategies that work beyond the research period. We introduce Quant-Arena, a benchmark and public leaderboard for end-to-end quantitative research on real A-share market data. A model constructs features and stock-scoring code, receives feedback only on 2022--2023, and freezes one strategy for assessment on the sealed year 2024. A cash-account evaluator applies trading constraints and costs, including market impact estimated from a simulated order book. Scores are dominated by realized excess return and complemented by signal quality and the diversity of features used by the strategy. We evaluate nine frontier models, among which GPT-6 Astra scores highest; yet no model beats the equal-weight market in 2024. Quant-Arena is open to submissions from any model or agent harness.

cross The End of Consensus Research?

Authors: Cl\'ement Burgelin, Rachid Guerraoui, Zicong Hong, Yinan Hu, Anne-Marie Kermarrec, Rafael Pires, Rasmus Moorits Veski, Martijn de Vos

Abstract: Consensus is a fundamental problem in distributed computing and at the core of state machine replication (SMR). Despite decades of active human research, discovering practical low-latency solutions to the problem is still open, as it requires combining protocol knowledge, correctness reasoning, implementation, and experimental evaluation. We report on how AI helped us discover A-greement, a new consensus protocol for geo-replicated state machines that significantly outperforms state-of-the-art solutions. Averaged across six deployments with 5 or 9 replicas, A-greement reduces the mean latency of a geo-distributed key-value store over the fastest baseline by 12.2% and 33.3% on write-heavy and read-heavy workloads, respectively, and matches the fastest baseline on read-only workloads. A-greement combines lease-authorized local reads, direct client learning for leader-ordered writes, and a fast fixed-quorum per-key write path, selected according to network topology and workload. At the heart of our discovery lies AI-greement, an agent harness for the "autonomous" discovery, implementation, experimentation, and verification of candidate protocols. AI-greement integrates (1) a concept-oriented protocol knowledge base, (2) a modular implementation skeleton, and (3) a Lean-based verification framework with reusable safety definitions, reference specifications, and proofs. AI-greement is of independent interest: its Lean formalization uncovers several previously unreported vulnerabilities in existing consensus protocols, and it provides guidelines on how domain-specific harnesses can turn general-purpose coding agents into effective tools for (distributed) computing research. Yet we argue that human consensus experts remain essential before, during, and after the search: agents reshape the researcher's role rather than end consensus research.

cross Environment Duels: A Microcosm of LLM Co-Evolution

Authors: Shibo Jin, Zhiqiu Xu, Yiling Zou, Shangyu Gong, Mayur Naik

Abstract: Through a tangle of real-world interactions, from synthetic training pipelines to data shared on the open web, frontier LLMs absorb what their predecessors and competitors create. How well does each model play its role in this ecosystem, as a creator and as a solver? We introduce Environment Duels, a dynamic agentic benchmark that evaluates both capabilities on interactive environments. Each model writes environments, each with a privileged hint, aiming for tasks that capable solvers fail without the hint and pass with it. Across nine frontier LLMs, solving and authoring prove to be distinct skills: the strongest solvers are not the strongest authors, while smaller models learn more from how others played and write the hardest environments and the most useful hints. Beyond the scores, the pairwise interactions form a microcosm of how models affect one another. Head-to-head outcomes are transitive and so yield a stable ranking; independent authors converge on the same designs; and in a reinforcement-learning study with Qwen3.8-27B, the learner's hint lift on an author's environments tracks how much training on those environments improves it on held-out reasoning and agentic benchmarks, whether the environments are its own or another model's. Each of these patterns has a counterpart in how models shape one another outside the duel. By staging these interactions under controlled conditions, Environment Duels offers a window onto how LLMs teach, challenge and learn from one another. Code is available at https://github.com/LLM-Duels/EnvDuels.

URLs: https://github.com/LLM-Duels/EnvDuels.

cross PhysiCalWorld: In-Context Physics Calibration for World Models

Authors: Yunhao Luo, Wentao He, Yinpei Dai, Hyelin Nam, Hongze Fu, Joyce Chai, Nima Fazeli, Jeong Joon Park

Abstract: Existing world models typically perceive a scene through current visual frame or a narrow history window. However, these observations may not reveal the physics properties that determine transition dynamics, such as an object's mass distribution, joint damping, or surface friction. Without calibrating these physics factors for world models, predictions tend to regress toward an empirical prior and induce severe drift in novel test scenes. To this end, we propose PhysiCalWorld, which calibrates action-conditioned world models to the instance-specific dynamics via in-context prompting, without fine-tuning or analytical physics modeling. Our method encodes an exploratory interaction clip collected within the test scene into a compact physics descriptor token, which grounds subsequent predictions in the scene's actual physical behavior. PhysiCalWorld is trained end-to-end with the latent prediction loss alone, without physics labels. At inference time, the descriptor token is extracted once and can be reused across rollouts with negligible compute overhead. We evaluate PhysiCalWorld across four environments covering diverse physics variations and show that it substantially improves rollout prediction accuracy and visual planning success rate. The learned descriptors also form a physically structured latent space and generalize beyond the physics ranges seen during training.

cross Think Harder Where It Matters: Item-Level Adaptive Recursion for Sequential Recommendation

Authors: Zhanshuo Cao, Jiakai Tang, Yuqi Zhou, Sunhao Dai, Jun Xu

Abstract: Existing sequential recommenders usually allocate a fixed computational budget to every item in a user's interaction history, regardless of the item's contribution to the final prediction. We challenge this de facto design with two diagnostic findings. (1) Non-uniform computational need: items within the same sequence vary drastically in how much computation they require. Most converge after shallow processing while a critical minority only stabilizes at full depth. (2) Suboptimality of fixed allocation: given oracle access to the target item, a non-uniform depth assignment consistently outranks the fixed one, indicating that existing uniform computation both wastes capacity on already-converged items and sacrifices overall recommendation quality. These observations motivate a shift from fixed-depth computation to item-level adaptive reasoning. To this end, we propose LoopRec. LoopRec recursively applies a single weight-shared block and introduces a lightweight gate at each recursion boundary that makes a binary exit-or-continue decision for every item independently. Items whose representations have sufficiently matured exit early, while those requiring deeper reasoning continue to subsequent recursions. This design converts the fixed global depth into a learned, per-item property without auxiliary depth labels, and is readily adaptable to diverse backbone architectures. Extensive experiments on public benchmarks with four backbone families demonstrate that LoopRec consistently outperforms matched fixed-depth counterparts. Further analysis reveals that LoopRec's learned allocation exhibits interpretable patterns aligned with our post-hoc oracle analysis: lower-frequency items and more recent interactions naturally receive deeper computation. Our code is available at https://github.com/Elpmis117/LoopRec.

URLs: https://github.com/Elpmis117/LoopRec.

replace Generative Artificial Intelligence for Internet of Things Computing: A Systematic Survey

Authors: Fabrizio Mangione, Claudio Savaglio, Giancarlo Fortino

Abstract: The integration of Generative Artificial Intelligence (GenAI) within the Internet of Things (IoT) is garnering considerable interest. This growing attention stems from the continuous evolution and widespread adoption they are both having individually, enough to spontaneously reshape numerous sectors, including Healthcare, Manufacturing, and Smart Cities. Hence, their increasing popularity has catalyzed further extensive research for understanding the potential of the duo GenAI-IoT, how they interplay, and to which extent their synergy can innovate the state-of-the-art in their individual scenarios. However, despite the increasing prominence of GenAI for IoT Computing, much of the existing research remains focused on specific, narrowly scoped applications. This fragmented approach highlights the need for a more comprehensive analysis of the potential, challenges, and implications of GenAI integration within the broader IoT ecosystem. This survey exactly aims to address this gap by providing a holistic overview of the opportunities, issues, and considerations arising from the convergence of these mainstream paradigms. Our contribution is realized through a systematic literature review following the PRISMA methodology. replace PRISM: Prompt-Refined In-Context System Modelling for Financial Retrieval

Authors: Chun Chet Ng, Jia Yu Lim, Zhen Hao Chu, Yixi Zhou, Wei Zeng Low

Abstract: Deploying LLMs for financial information retrieval demands accuracy, cost efficiency, and reproducibility, yet most approaches require expensive fine-tuning. PRISM is a training-free framework that composes three deployable modules (prompt engineering, in-context learning, and a candidate refinement module) with a boundary-condition study of multi-agent coordination for financial document retrieval. On FiQA-2018, stacked reranking and $k$-reciprocal encoding raise Recall@100 from 0.7783 to 0.8326. On FinAgentBench, a Cohere-refined top-50 pool yields a higher observed end-to-end NDCG@5, below this evaluation's minimum detectable effect, while cutting per-query cost by about 35%. Added complexity does not always pay off: $k$-reciprocal encoding stops helping once the upstream stage is already high-recall, and multi-agent coordination is not a default for fine-grained chunk ranking. Matched pairs that hold prompt, model, and exemplars fixed lose 0.0702 and 0.0681 combined NDCG@5 to their single-path controls, and the whole gap sits at the chunk stage. We release an expert-annotated FinAgentBench evaluation set, together with a label-robustness sweep over 16 alternative ground-truth constructions, restoring a reproducible evaluation path after the original evaluator went offline. Without fine-tuning, PRISM placed third on the official FinAgentBench private leaderboard (leaderboard basis, NDCG@5 0.71181), reaches NDCG@10 0.6197 on FiQA-2018, and 98% accuracy on FinanceBench. We report per-query latency, token, and dollar accounting across roughly 40 configurations, present the quality-cost-latency Pareto frontier, and introduce FACET, a transparent SLA-specific scalarization for choosing among non-dominated configurations. Code and dataset are released at https://bit.ly/prism-ailens.

URLs: https://bit.ly/prism-ailens.

replace Synthesis and Verification of Register Automata over Data Sequences (Technical Report)

Authors: Chih-Duo Hong, Hongjian Jiang, Anthony W. Lin, Oliver Markgraf, Julian Parsert, Tony Tan

Abstract: Data sequences, such as time series, traces, and sensor streams, are words over large or infinite data domains. Their properties often depend on comparisons between values at different positions, which finite automata cannot express directly. We study synthesis and verification for Deterministic Register Automata (DRAs) over ordered data sequences. DRAs combine finite control with registers for remembered data values, providing compact symbolic descriptions of quantitative sequence languages. Our technical core is a local-search synthesis procedure and an exact robustness checker for DRAs. We fix the alphabet theory to ordered rationals $(\Q;<,=)$ with rational guard constants and use non-negative affine accumulator costs. For a fixed number of DRA registers, the checker runs in polynomial time; its dependence on that number is exponential. It reduces robustness to coverability and then to a shortest-path problem. Since the search ranges over $\Q^+$, it produces variable-length witnesses and, for RAA-definable alignment costs such as DTW, explores a counterexample space with no a priori length bound. As an application, we use learned DRAs as interpretable surrogates for queried neural sequence classifiers. A probably approximately correct (PAC) validation loop refines rejected candidates, returns genuine non-robustness witnesses for the classifier when found, and accepts a surrogate only with statistical guarantees for fidelity and local stability. We implement the framework in $\textsc{RAExc}$, enabling the synthesis of interpretable automata and the verification of their robustness. We evaluate the approach on benchmark languages and sequentialized MNIST, demonstrating high accuracy while verifying the robustness of the learned automata.

replace Replayable Financial Agents: A Determinism-Faithfulness Assurance Harness for Tool-Using LLM Agents

Authors: Raffi Khatchadourian

Abstract: Replaying a financial agent requires specifying the conditions of a rerun, preserving its execution record, and deciding which parts of its behavior to compare. We introduce the Determinism-Faithfulness Assurance Harness (DFAH), a framework and practical protocol for organizing these choices. The framework distinguishes comparisons of final decisions, tool-name sets, and ordered tool names, while treating support from available evidence as a separate evaluation target. We illustrate the framework using 2,957 recorded executions in synthetic compliance-triage and data-operations tasks. One data-operations configuration repeated its recorded decision across all trials on 84% of cases, while its ordered tool-name sequence repeated on 26%. Conversely, some compliance cases retained the same tool sequence while recorded decisions changed. A case-level example shows how repeated labels accompanied different lookups and escalation calls. These observations demonstrate how the comparison level changes the description of repeated behavior. The resulting protocol connects configuration control, preservation of each attempt, comparison of records, and inspection of changed cases. DFAH provides a practical foundation for designing replay experiments and locating execution differences during the development and evaluation of financial agents.

replace A Theoretical Analysis of Why Masked Diffusion Models Mitigate the Reversal Curse

Authors: Moongyu Jeon, Sangwoo Shin, BumJun Kim, Kyelim Lee, Albert No

Abstract: Autoregressive language models (ARMs) suffer from the reversal curse: after learning ''$A$ is $B$,'' they often fail on the reverse query ''$B$ is $A$.'' Masked diffusion language models (MDMs) exhibit this failure in a much weaker form, but the underlying reason has remained unclear. A common explanation attributes this mitigation to their any-order masked training objective. However, observing ''$[\mathbf{M}]$ is $B$'' during training teaches recovery of $A$ from $B$ in one positional configuration, and does not by itself explain why the learned evidence should transfer to the reverse prompt ''$B$ is $[\mathbf{M}]$.'' We provide a theoretical analysis showing that this transfer arises from a parameter-level coupling between forward and reverse positional conditionals: shared Transformer parameters store token-pair evidence, while relative positional encodings route attention through queries and keys without changing the value-side evidence being retrieved. In a one-layer MDM, we prove that forward masked training strengthens evidence that is reusable in reverse queries, induces correlated forward--reverse attention routes, and yields a positively aligned shared-storage gradient component that decreases the reverse loss to first order. Controlled one-layer experiments and large-scale LLaDA/Dream experiments verify these signatures and show that they translate into improved reverse prediction.

replace 2-Step Agent: How a Bayesian Decision Maker Learns from AI-Decision Support

Authors: Otto Nyberg, Davide Tugnoli, Fausto Carcassi, Giovanni Cin\`a

Abstract: Prognostic predictions from ML models support human decision making in several fields, including high-stakes ones such as healthcare and the judiciary. Yet, we still lack a clear understanding of how decision makers learn from ML-based decision support (ML-DS). In this paper, we introduce a general computational framework, the 2-Step Agent, to capture how a prediction from an ML model can also be used as a source of useful information about the training data and the causal mechanism that generated it. Our framework models (i) how a prediction from an ML model affects the beliefs of a rational Bayesian agent, and (ii) how this change in beliefs affects the estimation of the causal effect, the downstream decision for a new observation, and the subsequent outcome. In addition to the framework itself, we make three contributions. First, for the multivariate linear-Gaussian setting, we derive a tractable solution for the Bayesian inference problem introduced, i.e. one in which the agent infers information about a population from an ML prediction. Second, we experimentally identify conditions under which ML-DS is beneficial compared to unassisted decision making. Third, we show that a single misaligned prior belief of the user can be sufficient for ML-DS to lead to worse downstream outcomes compared to no decision support even when the prognostic model is well-specified and the agent is perfectly rational. Hence, even under ideal conditions, ML-DS can do more harm than good.

replace The Alien Space of Science: Sampling Coherent but Cognitively Unavailable Research Directions

Authors: Alejandro H. Artiles, Martin Weiss, Levin Brinkmann, Iyad Rahwan, Bernhard Sch\"olkopf, Christopher Pal, Hugo Larochelle, Anirudh Goyal, Nasim Rahaman

Abstract: Scientific discovery is constrained not only by what is true, but by what is cognitively available to researchers. Many directions are coherent in light of the literature yet unlikely to be proposed because no existing community combines the relevant concepts, methods, and intuitions. Modern language models inherit this bias, recombining high-density regions of the literature when prompted for novel ideas. We target the complementary region, the alien space of science: directions that are scientifically plausible but unlikely under the distribution of existing researchers. Our method decomposes papers into conceptual units and clusters them into a vocabulary of idea atoms. A coherence model scores whether atom combinations form viable research directions, while an availability model scores whether any existing author community is positioned to produce them. We rank combinations to maximize coherence while minimizing availability. On 16,068 peer-reviewed LLM papers from NeurIPS, ICLR, ICML, and major NLP venues, the sampler produces more diverse research portfolios than frontier LLM baselines, including batch-prompted variants that see their entire output, while matching or exceeding their per-idea quality under blind human, LLM, and downstream experimental evaluation. In temporal validation, all components use only pre-2025 data. Selecting less than 1% of the candidate space recovers 2025 combinations at 35.9 times chance; weighting unavailability increases recovery of strictly novel combinations to 3.24 times chance. By separating scientific plausibility from community availability, our framework points toward AI ideation that complements rather than merely accelerates human science, expanding exploration into coherent directions the community may overlook.

replace Accurate and Efficient Multi-Channel Time Series Forecasting via Sparse Attention Mechanism

Authors: Lei Gao, Hengda Bao, Jingfei Fang, Guangzheng Wu, Weihua Zhou, Yun Zhou

Abstract: The task of multi-channel time series forecasting is ubiquitous in numerous fields such as finance, supply chain management, and energy planning. It is critical to effectively capture complex dynamic dependencies within and between channels for accurate predictions. This paper proposes Linear-Network (Li-Net), a novel architecture designed for multi-channel time series forecasting that captures the linear and non-linear dependencies among channels. Li-Net dynamically compresses representations across sequence and channel dimensions, processes the information through a configurable non-linear module and subsequently reconstructs the forecasts. Moreover, Li-Net integrates a sparse Top-K Softmax attention mechanism within a multi-scale projection framework to address these challenges. A core innovation is its ability to seamlessly incorporate and fuse multi-modal embeddings, guiding the sparse attention process to focus on the most informative time steps and feature channels. Through the experiment results on multiple real-world benchmark datasets demonstrate that Li-Net achieves competitive performance compared to state-of-the-art baseline methods. Furthermore, Li-Net provides a superior balance between prediction accuracy and computational burden, exhibiting significantly lower memory usage and faster inference times. Detailed ablation studies and parameter sensitivity analyses validate the effectiveness of each key component in our proposed architecture. replace FactReview: Evidence-Grounded Peer Review with Execution-Based Claim Verification

Authors: Ling Yue, Chaoqian Ouyang, Ruijun Huang, Hang Xu, Lu Li, Libin Zheng, Yuchen Liu, Wei Liu, Shaowu Pan, Shimin Di, Min-Ling Zhang

Abstract: AI is accelerating scientific research, but providing timely and trustworthy feedback remains challenging. Such feedback requires evaluating specific claims against verified evidence, helping researchers and autonomous research systems determine which findings can guide subsequent work. Thorough verification is time-consuming, and reviewers under time pressure may overlook or misjudge critical issues. Existing AI reviewers have yet to fill this gap because they often assess claims without adequately verifying the supporting evidence. We introduce FactReview, a framework that verifies claims in papers to provide more trustworthy feedback. It checks claims against the literature and examines derivations, while also executing the paper's code to test empirical claims. replace Adaptive auditing of AI systems with anytime-valid guarantees

Authors: Siyu Zhou, Patrick Vossler, Venkatesh Sivaraman, Yifan Mai, Jean Feng

Abstract: A major bottleneck in characterizing the failure modes of generative AI systems is the cost and time of annotation and evaluation. Consequently, adaptive testing paradigms have gained popularity, where one opportunistically decides which cases and how many to annotate based on past results. While this framework is highly practical, its extreme flexibility makes it difficult to draw statistically rigorous conclusions, as it violates classical assumptions: the number of observations is typically limited (often 10 to 50 cases) and decisions regarding sampling and stopping are made in the midst of data collection rather than based a pre-specified rule. To characterize what statistical inferences can be drawn from highly adaptive audits, we introduce a hypothesis testing framework from two "dueling" perspectives: (i) the model's null that asserts there is no failure mode with performance below a target threshold versus (ii) the auditor's null that asserts they have a sampling strategy that will uncover a failure mode. Leveraging Safe Anytime-Valid Inference (SAVI), we formalize the auditor as conducting "testing by betting," which translates into simultaneous e-processes for testing the dueling null hypotheses. Furthermore, if the auditor is sufficiently powerful, we prove that these two hypotheses are asymptotically inverses of each other, in that passage of such an audit certifies the AI system as being globally robust. Empirically, we demonstrate that our proposed testing procedures maintain anytime-valid Type-I error control, outperform pre-specified testing methods, and can reach statistically rigorous conclusions sometimes with as few as 20 observations.

replace WindINR: Latent-State INR for Fast Local Wind Query and Correction in Complex Terrain

Authors: Yi Xiao, Qilong Jia, Hang Fan, Pascal Fua, Robert Jenssen, Xiaosong Ma, Wei Xue

Abstract: Many decisions in complex terrain require fast wind estimates at specific locations and heights rather than dense forecasts on fixed grids. We present WindINR, a latent-state implicit neural representation framework for continuous high-resolution local wind queries and correction using sparse observations. A latent-conditioned decoder maps terrain descriptors, a low-resolution background field, and continuous query coordinates to local wind estimates. WindINR separates reusable representation learning from sample-specific latent correction. During training, a privileged encoder infers reference latent states from high-resolution supervision, while a deployable predictor estimates initial states from inference-time inputs. Their discrepancies define a dataset-adaptive Gaussian prior over latent corrections. At inference time, network weights remain fixed, and only the latent state is updated through a regularized objective incorporating sparse observations and their uncertainty. We train WindINR on the large-scale FuXi-CFD dataset and evaluate it using controlled observing system simulation experiments, simulated helicopter trajectories, and real UAV measurements over Troms{\o}. Across these settings, sparse-observation assimilation consistently improves local wind predictions, with WindINR outperforming several baselines, including WindSeer. Updating only a low-dimensional latent state yields a 14-fold speedup over full-network fine-tuning while preserving continuous queries at arbitrary spatial coordinates. WindINR thus provides an efficient interface between kilometer-scale background products, sparse local observations, and local wind queries in complex terrain.

replace Proposals and challenges for zero-knowledge verification of frontier AI training

Authors: Pierre Peign\'e, Ky Nguyen, Paul Wang

Abstract: Proposals for Frontier AI rules often use cumulative training compute as the main criterion for identifying high-impact models. Recent governance analyses call zero-knowledge proofs promising but currently impractical at frontier scale [26, 4]. We argue this barrier reflects the current paradigm, not a fundamental limit. Our frontier dense-pre-training design combines a pre-committed specification, inter-node network observations, and live Merkle commitments to intermediate computation. Our reference zero-knowledge virtual machine (zkVM) provides a succinct proof layer with native BF16/FP32 precompiles. We qualitatively compare zkVMs, custom circuits, and direct interactive audits by transferability, soundness, and cost. Public randomness beacons are a separate audit-selection choice. At challenged positions, proofs check the committed computation against declared BF16/FP32 semantics bit-for-bit. A private training specification targets model-architecture confidentiality. Genesis proofs cover initialisation; then, in-training step proofs verify selected steps across the run. Ex-ante attestations enforce policy-relevant claims as running invariants on every proved step. Together they create a governance-auditable training record. Detection-probability formulas quantify sampling guarantees from the sampling budget and the share of computation altered. We give numerical examples and explain what sampling does not cover. We note, however, that weight smuggling could reuse or merge weights trained elsewhere without charging that work to the current run. We then argue that regimes with global training budgets are more robust than regimes with per-run FLOP caps. This advantage depends on counting all contributing training. We catalogue remaining research and engineering problems for external contributors.

replace Lean4Agent: Formal Modeling and Verification for Agent Workflow and Trajectory

Authors: Ruida Wang, Jerry Huang, Pengcheng Wang, Xuanqing Liu, Luyang Kong, Tong Zhang

Abstract: Equipping Large Language Models (LLMs) to execute reliable multi-step workflows has become a central challenge in artificial intelligence. Despite recent advances in LLMs' agentic capabilities, most agent systems still lack formal methods for specifying, verifying, and debugging their workflow and execution trajectories. This challenge mirrors a long-standing problem in mathematics, where the ambiguity of natural languages (NLs) motivates the development of formal languages (FLs). Inspired by this paradigm, we propose **Lean4Agent**, to the best of our knowledge, the first framework that uses Lean4, a dependent-type FL to model and verify agent behavior. **Lean4Agent** launches **FormalAgentLib**, an extensible Lean4 library for formally modeling and verifying agent workflows' semantic consistency under explicit assumptions, and enabling localization of execution-time failures revealed by trajectories. Building on **FormalAgentLib**, we further develop **LeanEvolve**, which applies results in **FormalAgentLib** to revise workflows to enhance its capability. Extensive experiments on a hard problem subset of SWE-Bench-Verified and a subset of ELAIP-Bench across 5 leading LLMs indicate that the verification-passing workflows outperform the failing ones by an average of **11.94%**, and **LeanEvolve** further improves SWE performance by **7.47%** on average. Furthermore, **Lean4Agent** establishes a foundation for a new field of using expressive dependent-type FL to formally model and verify agent behavior.

replace Rethinking Synthetic Rationale Supervision for Disease Prediction

Authors: Buxin Su, Bingxuan Li, Cheng Qian, Yiwei Wang, Jin Jin, Bingxin Zhao

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning with synthetic rationales is widely assumed to improve clinical prediction by teaching models not only what to predict but why. We test this assumption on five-year Alzheimer's disease and related dementias (ADRD) prediction from longitudinal health histories. In this setting, we generate training rationales using the correct labels, then fine-tune models to output a rationale followed by a label. Across a large-scale controlled experiment of 504 configurations, we find that Rationale First SFT consistently and substantially hurts predictive performance relative to Label Only fine-tuning. The degradation persists across model families and data scales, and is not resolved by using a reasoning-oriented base model. A fixed-protocol cardiovascular disease (CVD) validation reproduces similar findings. Crucially, the failure is not explained by poor rationale quality: human expert annotation confirms that the generated rationales are medically accurate and faithfully grounded in patient-specific evidence, and few-shot experiments show that the same rationales improve performance when used as inference-time demonstrations rather than training targets. A simple label-ordering intervention clarifies the mechanism: placing the prediction before the same rationale restores predictive performance. This suggests that conditioning predictions on generated rationales contributes to the degradation. We hope our work paves the way toward a more precise understanding of when and how rationale-based supervision helps and when it does not, guiding the responsible development of language models for high-stakes clinical prediction.

replace TNODEV: Toolbox for Neural ODE Verification

Authors: Abdelrahman Sayed Sayed, Pierre-Jean Meyer, Mohamed Ghazel

Abstract: Neural ordinary differential equations (neural ODE) gained attention in safety critical settings such as continuous-time controllers for cyber-physical systems and classifiers integrated into automated decision pipelines, raising the question whether their behavior can be formally verified. Existing tools dedicated to neural ODE provide only a single reachability call without iterative input-set refinement, limiting the precision of their verdicts to whatever one reachability call can deliver. We present TNODEV, the first formal verifier for neural ODE that integrates a falsification checker, a fast interval-based reachability backend based on continuous-time mixed monotonicity, a verification and refinement loop with three input-set splitting heuristics, and a parallel scheduler in a single end-to-end pipeline. TNODEV supports safe-set inclusion verification on pure neural ODE, neural ODE in closed loop with a neural network controller and general neural ODE (GNODE), with the safe set specified either as an interval or as the half-space intersection induced by a target classification label. We evaluate TNODEV on a range of benchmarks across safe-set inclusion and classification-robustness properties, including a direct reachability comparison against NNV 2.0 and CORA and a verification comparison against NNV 2.0 on MNIST general neural ODE classifiers.

replace Beyond Accuracy: Measuring Logical Compliance of Predictive Models

Authors: Guillaume Olivier Delplanque (TYREX), Pierre Genev\`es (TYREX), Nabil Laya\"ida (TYREX)

Abstract: Machine learning models are predominantly evaluated through predictive performance metrics such as ranking quality, prediction error, or classification accuracy. While these metrics effectively quantify how closely predictions match the ground truth, they do not assess whether model outputs respect predefined logical or domain-specific constraints. In high-stakes applications, including healthcare, finance, and autonomous systems, logical consistency can be as critical as predictive accuracy, yet no standard metric captures this dimension. We introduce the Rule Violation Score (RVS), a complementary evaluation metric that quantifies the extent to which a predictive model respects a given set of logical rules, independently of predictive accuracy. RVS treats hard rules (strict constraints) and soft rules (statistical regularities) differently, can be evaluated on any dataset and on any predictive model expressed over a relational vocabulary, and can be computed using SQL queries that are automatically generated for Horn rules. Beyond evaluating models, RVS can also evaluate the logical consistency of training datasets and help identify poorly defined rules. We evaluate RVS on three benchmarks covering knowledge graph link prediction and relational regression, including rule-based, embedding-based, and neuro-symbolic predictive models. Our results demonstrate that two models achieving comparable predictive accuracy can exhibit substantially different levels of logical compliance, revealing differences in model behavior that standard metrics fail to capture.

replace DFAH-Bench: What Agreement Leaves Unmeasured in Financial Agents

Authors: Raffi Khatchadourian

Abstract: When a financial agent repeats its decision, how much does that tell us about the actions behind it? DFAH-Bench examines this question through replay experiments, a controlled test of omitted tool calls, and banking tasks with authorization checks. Across 570 eligible prospective replay episodes, decisions agreed at rates of 94-95%, while tool paths, including inputs and results, agreed at 45-52%; one condition fell one group short of the planned minimum. In a separate 48-execution diagnostic, deleting the same calls from repeated records left agreement perfect. In banking probes, repeated authorization decisions sometimes conflicted with constructed rule labels. The $\tau$-Knowledge banking study scheduled 1,080 episodes, with 1,033 known outcomes. Structural checks alone completed more primary tasks than either model-based gate-and-recovery bundle. Input limits and recovery behavior materially shape these results. The typed-choice bundle had lower mean episode cost than the generative bundle on complete task pairs, but fewer successes across the primary schedule under every assignment of unknown outcomes. Missing outcomes prevented the planned confirmatory tests, so the banking comparisons are descriptive. Together, the findings show that agreement alone cannot establish reliability: it can conceal changes in execution, systematic omissions, and unsupported decisions. Evaluation therefore needs to connect what the agent did, the evidence and rules governing its action, and whether it completed the task.

replace DeepLook: Uncertainty-Guided Branching with Lookahead Confidence for Efficient Reasoning

Authors: Tingxin Yang, Zefeng Wang, Mengyue Wang, Jinhe Bi, Xingcheng Zhou, Volker Tresp, Yunpu Ma

Abstract: Test-time scaling improves large language model reasoning, but spends compute uniformly along a reasoning trace, even in stretches that are already stable. Motivated by the observation that reasoning failures show an early onset of uncertainty, we introduce DeepLook, a training-free decoding framework that monitors segment-level confidence, branches only at uncertainty bottlenecks, and ranks candidate continuations by Average Lookahead Confidence (ALC)---their mean confidence over fixed-horizon rollouts---before completing and voting over a pruned subset. On four competition mathematics benchmarks across four open models, DeepLook matches or exceeds the accuracy of DeepConf-low in 12 of 16 settings while generating 87.3% fewer tokens, showing that a model's own confidence is informative enough to decide where extra decoding compute is worth spending. On knowledge-intensive GPQA-Diamond the accuracy advantage disappears even though the token saving does not: coherent hallucinations also receive high ALC, so internal confidence localizes instability, not falsehood. Code is available at https://github.com/txyang1/deeplook.

URLs: https://github.com/txyang1/deeplook.

replace CTBench: Evaluating Troubleshooting Capabilities of AI Agents in Realistic Telecom Network Operations

Authors: Xingyu Yan, Tingting Dai, Antonio De Domenico, Mohamed Sana, Nicola Piovesan, Changchang Li, Bowen Liu, Kun Jiang, Mengjie Zhang, Dingcheng Shan, Jing-Cheng Pang, Chenwei Wu, Sijie Wu, Lianying Chao, Haoran Cai, Jiantao Ye, Xubin Li, Simon Mark Lucas, Xin Chen

Abstract: While autonomous agents show promise for network operations, existing telecom troubleshooting evaluations fail to capture the partial observability and heterogeneity of real-world telecom environments. We introduce CTBench, a public benchmark evaluating agent troubleshooting capabilities in root cause analysis (RCA) and path restoration. Constructed by domain experts, CTBench features rich metadata and golden evidence trajectories, utilizing metrics that assess both final accuracy and diagnostic reasoning. Our evaluation of state-of-the-art agents reveals that while they excel at path endpoint identification, they severely underperform in RCA. Crucially, even when agents produce correct answers, they frequently fail to gather the requisite diagnostic evidence demanded in operational practice. Finally, we demonstrate that increased computational expenditure does not guarantee improved diagnostic competence, highlighting critical gaps in current agent capabilities.

replace Toward Safe LLM Agents: A Survey of Specification, Verification, and Enforcement

Authors: Pierre Dantas, Lucas Cordeiro, Ehsan Nowroozi, Norbert Tihanyi

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents take irreversible real-world actions, yet no system gives formally grounded, task-level safety guarantees for their plans. In a PRISMA 2020 systematic review, we searched six databases (20-29 May 2026) for work on LLM agent specification, verification, and enforcement published in 2022-2026. We narratively synthesized 38 studies, assessed risk of bias per study, and graded evidence certainty with GRADE. Three authors independently screened a repeated search to validate the single-reviewer screening (agreement: Fleiss' $\kappa = 0.81$). First, specification is the bottleneck: on one benchmark, only 24.8-34.2% of LLM translations from natural language into Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL) are semantically correct. Second, runtime monitoring is the most mature enforcement strategy; it reduced unsafe actions by 65.37% in one household-agent study, but its guarantees cover only single steps or short horizons. Third, in one $\tau$-Bench study of transactional tool-use agents, blocking 94% of unsafe actions still left fewer than 5% of tasks completed both safely and correctly in most settings, because agents found other unsafe routes (the verifier tax). Fourth, no approach simultaneously achieves soundness, completeness, scalability, semantic grounding, and utility preservation. For seven of the nine principal claims, GRADE gives the lowest certainty rating (Very Low), meaning further studies are likely to produce substantially different figures; each rests on one or two studies in narrow domains. Two of the open problems we rank highest are translating requirements into formal specifications that keep their meaning and enforcing safety over whole plans rather than single actions. We contribute a three-level taxonomy and an agenda of ten open problems.

replace Language Models Give Group-Linked Fields the Wrong Weight

Authors: Zengqing Wu, Chuan Xiao

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used to judge people, and their answers can depend on inputs correlated with a protected attribute such as race, even when that attribute is never shown. Interpreting this reliance requires knowing how much predictive information those inputs add, but audits often report only whether a decision changes. We introduce an evidence-referenced audit that compares proxy reliance with two references, the known outcome rule and a Bayesian linear regression fitted to the same labeled examples, while measuring ranking accuracy separately. We apply it to four models in a simulated patient-ranking task with known field weights. We find four results. First, at zero predictive value, the models still relied on fields that carried no information, while the same-example learner did not. Second, as proxies became predictive, reliance grew far less than the reference. Third, display format mattered. Ordered labels drew more reliance than arbitrary labels, a direction that held in all four models. Fourth, ranking accuracy did not consistently track these changes in reliance. Based on these findings, we argue that decision changes and accuracy cannot show whether proxy reliance is warranted by the evidence. Audits should report reliance against an explicit predictive reference, alongside accuracy. Ordered fields whose values move with group membership, such as scores or income bands, are a useful starting point. Together, these results make it possible to measure how far reliance departs from the evidence, rather than infer it from observed choices alone.

replace From Atomic to Agentic: Towards Interpretable Evaluation of LLMs' Agentic Mathematical Capabilities

Authors: Jiayi Kuang, Yinghui Li, Yunze Song, Keyu Chen, Zhifeng Shen, Yangning Li, Yidong Wang, Di Yin, Ruizhi Qiao, Xing Sun, Kai Jin, Ying Shen, Liang Lin, Philip S. Yu

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are evolving from performing end-to-end mathematical reasoning to integrating agentic intelligence. However, most existing math benchmarks evaluate only final answers. This outcome-oriented evaluation provides limited diagnostic value for identifying process-level failures or rigorous logic, failing to guide the transformation of LLMs into robust agents. To bridge this gap, we present a process-level benchmark designed to evaluate the inherent agentic mathematical reasoning abilities of LLMs. Our framework aligns problem-solving agentic behaviors with a structured taxonomy of reusable mathematical atomic capabilities. We design a comprehensive suite of planning, action, and feedback tasks across both textual and multimodal contexts, supported by an automated pipeline that synthesizes high-quality trajectories and produces fine-grained annotations via controlled LLM rewriting. Experiments reveal that models with similar end-to-end accuracy can exhibit markedly different agentic capability profiles. This demonstrates that process-level evaluation is crucial for interpreting the true potential of LLMs and guiding the development of next-generation mathematical agents.

replace Interface-Induced Trajectory Censoring

Authors: Wenbo Wang, Di Sang

Abstract: Tool-use evaluations typically measure calls through a serving stack, which can report zero calls even when model outputs contain call-shaped payloads. We study interface-induced trajectory censoring: a mismatch between model emissions and the interface that converts them into actions. On selected BFCL v4 subsets, changing only the serving adapter while holding model weights and other evaluation settings fixed moves scores under the official scorer from 0.00 to 0.96 on simple_python and from 0.00 to 0.19 on multi_turn_base. A template-parser factorial experiment shows strong complementarity: neither component replacement alone restores parsing from the baseline, whereas their joint replacement recovers 196/200 first-request parses. Across five Qwen2.5-Coder checkpoints, classifier-positive emissions increase with model size while the mismatched interface reports no calls; a separate matched-interface Qwen3 ladder exhibits few silent emissions through 8B. Experiments on 115 tau-bench retail tasks extend the mechanism to a stateful, non-code environment. In a preregistered, single-seed, 150-step 7B LoRA experiment, changing only the parser registered in verl increases tool executions from 10 to 16,844. However, neither a shared programmatic-feedback evaluation nor a post-hoc common native function-calling evaluation detects a held-out success gain from repair; rescue counts do not increase. Interface repair thus restores tool-mediated experience without a detected performance gain in this setting. These findings show that tool-use measurements depend on the model-interface configuration and motivate layer-wise observability and conformance checks before attributing evaluation or training outcomes to the model alone.

replace World Models Under Asynchronous Sensor Observations

Authors: Akash Anand, Abhay Anand, Yash Vishe

Abstract: Learned world models typically assume that observations arrive synchronously, an abstraction inherited from simulators that return a complete state vector at each environment step. Physical sensing instead operates at heterogeneous rates, leaving most observation channels stale at any given instant. Interpolating stale channels introduces measurements that were never observed, while downsampling to the slowest sensor discards valid measurements. A natural alternative is to zero-order-hold the most recent reading and provide the known sampling schedule to the model through two features, staleness and time-to-refresh. We test this prediction using transformer world models across three regimes of increasing causal coupling: open-loop rollouts in continuous-control locomotion, closed-loop model-predictive planning in which each learned model serves as the planner dynamics, and a linear latched-actuator system in which refresh events apply a zero-order-held command to the plant. Our findings show that the effectiveness of time-to-refresh depends on the causal role of the sampling schedule, specifically when refresh events affect the system rather than merely report its state. These results establish when sampling schedules provide useful information for predictive world models operating under asynchronous physical observations.

replace Adaptive Entangled Neurons in the Brain and Natural Intelligence Simulations for Artificial General Intelligence

Authors: Haochen Li, Xinshuai Guo, Jingdong Ouyang, Wei Zhang, Leilei Shi

Abstract: Understanding the brain's mechanisms is crucial in neuroscience, particularly for simulating natural intelligence and advancing artificial general intelligence (AGI). Liu et al. (2024) and Deco et al. (2025) propose that quantum entanglement may underlie the synchronization of millions of neurons, introducing the Liu-Chen-Ao (LCA) hypothesis of entangled nerve fibers. However, directly testing this idea remains challenging. Here we employ a nonlocal many-body wave equation, introducing a generalized probability-wave framework to model collective behaviors among adaptive, interacting agents, particles, or neurons. This approach enables testable eigenmodes, offering an indirect means of examining the LCA hypothesis by observing patterns in collective trading behaviors. These patterns, rooted in behavioral psychology, are thought to mirror the internal brain's decision-making and underlying mechanisms. Our analysis of Chinese intraday stock market data shows that adaptive entangled game modes explain 82%-94% of decision patterns, sharply contrasting neoclassical finance predictions assuming independent rational agents. Additionally, 2-12% of actions adapt to news, events, and environmental changes, marked by rapid equilibrium shifts, while truly independent behaviors make up less than 5%. These results empirically support the LCA hypothesis and underscore the need to simulate the brain's mechanisms and generate adaptive, entangled modules for AGI. They also expose the shortcomings of conventional artificial neural networks (ANNs) that depend on vast, opaque parameters. By combining ANN-based AI with probability-wave-based entangled brain simulations, machine learning enables the creation of human-like processing units (HPUs). Such HPUs promise more efficient and robust AGI, particularly for embodied intelligence and robotics.

replace Making AI-Assisted Claims Independently Challengeable: Publication Authority and a Protocol for Falsifiable Publication Records

Authors: Torsten Olivi Tiltack, Yifei Dong, Kun Yu, Xu Wang, Wei Liu, Jianlong Zhou, Ren Ping Liu, Fang Chen

Abstract: AI-assisted claims can appear authoritative when evidence, analysis, human authorization, presentation, and correction history describe different states. We develop Publication Authority: an exact-state, non-transferable, single-use publication capability within PAC-2026 (Publication-Accountability Calculus), an AIJIM Protocol candidate. The evaluated specification version (SF-4) defines six non-compensating obligations: evidence, runs and artifacts, measurement disclosure, authorization, surface correspondence, and lifecycle continuity. Each yields a target-bound witness, localized counterexample, or localized unverifiability. Only a fresh, complete all-pass record derives a permit consumed by one atomic publication transition. Ten models explored 110,764 distinct safe reachable states; 76 unsafe configurations yielded the expected violation or observer countermodel. A surface passing its correspondence check cannot authorize publication unless admitted by the accepted record. SF-4 separates evidence horizon from verification time and rejects authentic but causally invalid authorization. An in-house, instance-blind study (G7-B) matched 183 scored expectations after shared integration. Three author-led native comparisons identify gaps in a constructed in-toto/OPA profile: measurement-state admission, proof-expiry evaluation, and required-run identity. Repairs restore parity on the tested cases. An exploratory extension constructs exact claim-edit regions under existing e-closure semantics: a minimum retained core qualifies every permitted selection. For the full hypothesis universe (U=H), a quadratic frontier computes all strongest-core sizes. Enumeration and native controls distinguish reusable statistical qualification from the fresh publication authority each candidate requires. Results yield transferable protection rules; independent conformance and field evaluation remain open.

replace An auditable conditional-strategy framework for open-ended decision-making in complex lung cancer

Authors: Daoyun Wang, Zhicheng Huang, Huaiyuan Sun, Jiaqi Xu, Xiaowei Xu, Zhibo Zheng, Zhongxing Bing, Yuxiao Lin, Yicheng Liang, Chao Gao, Bowen Xue, Kai Zhang, Song Xu, Wanpu Yan, Hui Xia, Lin Li, Xiang Yan, Mu Hu, Qianli Ma, Zhiqiang Xue, Xiaofang Liu, Zhihai Han, Nan Zhang, Chuanhao Tang, Tongmei Zhang, Lan Song, Zhaohui Zhu, Xuan Zeng, Shafei Wu, Hui Guan, Lei Deng, Huaxia Yang, Zeliang Lian, Wubin Sun, Yongxin Wang, Xiaohui Shen, Binlin Wang, Tiantian Gu, Yu Cui, Li Zhang, Shirui Wang, Naixin Liang

Abstract: Complex lung cancer decisions can involve several defensible pathways whose eligibility, sequencing and safety depend on unresolved information. Effective support must make explicit how patient conditions govern pathway eligibility, deferral and redirection. MedGPT Clinical Explorer (MCE) organizes alternatives, decision-changing unknowns, safety constraints and fallback into a conditional strategy for clinician review. To evaluate this representation in physician-authored strategies, multidisciplinary experts established case-specific references for 40 cases within a purposive 100-case corpus, and 250 physicians from 98 institutions produced 2,250 strategies under unaided, retrieval-reference and MCE-assisted conditions. MCE-assisted strategies expressed more applicable clinical requirements, measured by the Admissible Pathway Attainment Score (APAS; 0-100), than unaided strategies (adjusted difference, 12.87; 95% CI, 11.18-14.55) and retrieval-reference strategies (5.22; 3.52-6.93). With the same knowledge base available in the retrieval-reference and MCE-assisted conditions, the additional content centered on candidate pathways, decision-critical information and safety constraints. Physicians' whole-strategy acceptability judgments correlated with APAS (Spearman's rho = 0.671), while a complementary relationship audit assessed whether candidates, conditions and subsequent actions were coherently connected. Together, these findings identify two complementary dimensions of open-ended decision support: coverage of clinically relevant content and coherent links among pathways, conditions and subsequent actions. MCE provides a shared decision object that makes consequential omissions and pathway contingencies visible before action; prospective studies should evaluate its effects on clinical workflow and patient outcomes.

replace EHRAdapt: Adapting Pretrained Language Models to Electronic Health Records with Semantic Priors for Rare Clinical Events

Authors: Andre R Goncalves, Vincent Liu, Priyadip Ray

Abstract: Electronic health records (EHRs) encode clinical histories as (time, modality, code) tuples, whereas pretrained language models expect text tokens. Serializing tuples as text inflates sequence length and repeatedly encodes the same structure. We introduce EHRAdapt, an adapter that maps tuples directly into a frozen language model's embedding space. Modality and time gaps enter through a learned embedding and attention biases, and event codes receive dedicated vectors. Learning them is challenging because clinical vocabularies are long-tailed, leaving rare events with few observations. EHRAdapt therefore represents each event vector as the sum of a semantic prior and an evidence residual. The prior is a frozen, ontology-trained biomedical embedding of the event's clinical description, mapped into the input space by a shared learned projection, so it supplies clinical meaning even when observations are scarce. The residual is a learned low-rank, event-specific correction that refines it as evidence accumulates. We perform continued pretraining on records from about 4 million patients with three frozen LLM backbones, OLMo2 1B, Llama3.2 1B, and OLMo2 7B, training only the adapter (0.1--0.6% of all parameters). The full adapter outperforms all ablations in held-out next-event prediction on every backbone. Removing the semantic pathway hurts rare events most, over ten times more than the most frequent ones, whereas removing the residual hurts overall prediction but improves it for the rarest events. On reportable infectious-disease and syndromic classification, EHRAdapt outperforms text-based LLM and count-based baselines and the same LLMs adapted to the same records through next-event prediction, and both pathways improve rare-disease discrimination. The two pathways therefore play complementary roles, visible only when results are broken down by event frequency.

replace AdaGuard: An Adaptive Guard Model with User-defined Policies

Authors: Yunhao Feng, Yifan Ding, Yuxiang Xie, Zheng Li, Mingrui Lao, Zeyuan Wang, Yanming Guo

Abstract: Guard models support the safe deployment of language model agents, but fixed risk taxonomies limit their ability to accommodate requirements that vary across applications and tasks. Under user-defined policies, detecting violations requires interpreting both the applicable rules and the agent's behavior, since identical actions can receive different judgments under different policies. To support learning this capability, we introduce AdaptiveSafety, a dataset of 10,939 training examples and 1,000 test examples covering policies with 1--100 rules. The dataset combines trajectories from multiple sources with policy and behavioral counterfactuals, pairing each example with an explanation and the complete set of violated rules. These counterfactuals expose changes that alter compliance, while structural augmentations provide supervision for consistency under rule reordering and identifier remapping. Building on this supervision, we propose SafePO, a reinforcement learning algorithm for refining violation identification while balancing explanatory reasoning and final verdicts. SafePO uses structured rewards to assess prediction correctness, retains group-relative advantages at the response level, and employs a separately trained value model to modulate token weights within explanation and verdict regions. Separate normalization controls their relative contribution to training despite differences in length. Through supervised initialization followed by SafePO, we develop AdaGuard, a family of 0.6B, 4B, and 8B guard models that assess agent trajectories under policies supplied at inference time. Our 4B model achieves binary accuracies of 89.30\% on AdaptiveSafety and 71.82\% on DynaBench. The project repository is available at https://github.com/Yunhao-Feng/AdaGuard

URLs: https://github.com/Yunhao-Feng/AdaGuard

replace Predictor-Guided Latent Space Codon Optimization for Maximizing Protein Expression

Authors: Alberto Caron, Tianyu Cui, Dmytro S. Lituiev, Mangal Prakash, Artem Moskalev, Amina Mollaysa, Bo Zhai, Hirsh Nanda, Daniel M. Poole, Zhongyin Liu, Iman Farasat, Robert Davidson, Nikolay V. Manyakov, Tommaso Mansi, Scott Oloff, Rui Liao

Abstract: Codon optimization, the process of selecting synonymous codons to improve mRNA translation efficiency and protein expression, is central to therapeutic protein production and mRNA vaccines, yet it remains a hard problem. The design space is discrete and combinatorially large, precluding gradient-based methods, and existing tools rely on heuristic proxies (e.g., Codon Adaptation Index or GC-content) that poorly capture true expression. We introduce Latent-Space Codon Optimization (LSCO), which recasts this discrete problem as a continuous one by mapping sequences into the latent space of a pretrained mRNA language model, enabling efficient gradient-based search. LSCO combines four components: a data-driven expression objective from an uncertainty-aware predictor, a Minimum-Free-Energy regularizer for structural stability, a naturalness prior from a protein-to-codon back-translation model, and constrained decoding for protein fidelity. On a real-world, wet-lab antibody expression dataset, LSCO outperforms simple frequency-based, as well as modern deep generative baselines in predicted expression, while retaining suitable biophysical properties.

replace RocketAgent: A Long-Horizon Engineering Agent for Multidisciplinary Design of Liquid-Rocket Thrust Chambers

Authors: Junxiang He, Runze Mao, Kun He, Teng Zhang, Liming Zheng, Ke Xiao, Zhi X. Chen

Abstract: Liquid-rocket thrust-chamber design involves interdependent analyses in which downstream constraints can require earlier design decisions to be revisited. replace SciExam for ENSO: Can AI Agents Build Climate Models?

Authors: Yinling Zhang, Langchen Liu, Dongbin Xiu, Xueyan Zou, Xu Kuang, Mengdi Wang, Shilong Liu

Abstract: Language-model agents are increasingly asked to carry out open-ended scientific research, yet their results are usually graded against a known answer, a rubric, or a language-model reviewer, none of which can tell whether a new scientific model is valid. The AI Science Exam for El Nino-Southern Oscillation (SciExam for ENSO) is a benchmark in which agents build low-order stochastic models of ENSO, the dominant mode of interannual climate variability, from real observations. Within a six-hour budget, agents process the observations, write their own diagnostics, which are then frozen, and develop a model using only these diagnostics as feedback. Hidden graders then test whether the model reproduces ENSO's statistics, recovers unobserved variables, and forecasts held-out years, and score a published model in the same way. Across twelve agent systems, six produce models that score higher than the published model, mainly through better reconstruction and forecasting. The simplified forms of the stronger models are each compatible with one of the two competing explanations of ENSO's warm-cold asymmetry, an open debate that the task never mentions. Controlled runs of the top system under varied information suggest that its scores do not come from recalling the dated observational record and that the information it receives shapes how it builds its model. SciExam for ENSO can thus evaluate agent research where no answer is known, and the results suggest that agents can already build competitive models whose structures bear on questions that scientists still debate.

replace Estimating Great Expectations Under Autoregressive Language Models with Potentials

Authors: Francesco I. Re, Shubhangi Ghosh, Tim Vieira, Ryan Cotterell

Abstract: Many applications of language models require estimating the expectation of a test functional under the model. Obtaining precise estimates can be computationally expensive. We show how to improve estimation precision by exploiting next-token conditional probabilities available as a by-product of sampling. Our approach uses potentials: real-valued functions of prefixes that induce an additive decomposition of the test functional. We construct a family of estimators parameterized by these potentials and derive conditions for variance reduction. Across applications including entropy estimation, watermark detection, and rare-event probability estimation, our estimators achieve substantial variance reductions relative to Monte Carlo and importance sampling baselines at comparable computational cost.

replace HiPPT: Physiology-Grounded Wearable PPG Representation Learning via Multi-Scope Text Alignment

Authors: Jiaqi Wang, Wei Chen, Xiaoyu Li, Lexian Xia, ShuaiLong Tang, Chenran Zhang, Donglin Xie, Guangpu Zhu, Yelei Li, Yi Zhou

Abstract: Reusable wearable PPG representations should capture local pulse morphology and broader physiological regularities. Existing pretraining objectives, however, usually apply supervision at the recording or pair level, overlooking that different concepts become reliable over different scopes of waveform evidence. We propose HiPPT, a physiology-grounded framework that matches each supervision source to its appropriate evidence scope. We construct a PPG-Text corpus of over 4 million wrist-worn recordings from 202K users, pairing recordings with waveform-derived morphology and metadata-derived context descriptions. HiPPT combines record-level morphology alignment, which grounds individual recordings in pulse characteristics, with cross-recording context alignment, which aggregates recordings sharing the same metadata tuple to capture context-consistent regularities. Waveform-guided text modeling further recovers masked contextual concepts from PPG patch representations, providing fine-grained waveform-text supervision. Across six downstream tasks spanning blood pressure, vascular stiffness, physiological attributes, and cross-dataset heart-rate estimation, HiPPT achieves the best result on the primary linear-probing metrics. The aligned space also supports zero-shot profiling of age group, sex, and BMI category, individually and jointly, with performance improving as more recordings are aggregated. replace MADDIE: Multi-Agent Drug Discovery Engine

Authors: Ye Liu, Reza Averly, Avery Meyer, Frazier N. Baker, Ruoxi Gao, Daniel Adu-Ampratwum, Xia Ning

Abstract: Drug discovery remains a resource-intensive and time-consuming process, involving billions of dollars and a decade of development to advance a drug to market. While many in silico tools have accelerated this process, operating them still demands substantial time, effort, and expertise from medicinal chemists. Autonomous systems built on Large Language Models (LLMs) have emerged as a promising solution, yet current designs limit their performance. Existing single-agent systems scale poorly to long-horizon, multi-step, and realistic tasks, whereas multi-agent systems typically execute predefined workflows that cannot capture the iterative, non-linear nature of a real drug discovery campaign. Here, we present MADDIE, a multi-agent drug discovery engine. Across 50 protein targets, MADDIE produces molecules satisfying key property requirements and their synthesis routes, outperforming existing agentic systems, generative models, and general-purpose LLMs by a wide margin. Our analysis reveals how these agents collaborate and that MADDIE can employ different strategies across targets to achieve the campaign's goal, while still being efficient in time and tokens.
